It's really just the... the staying engaged part that's hard, not the details themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I sit at my desk for hours trying to get through my grading. I don't feel like I need to get up and move around or anything like that. I'm usually pretty still, it's just my brain that's wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're unable to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wouldn't say I feel anxious or worried. It's more just... frustration. I get annoyed with myself because I know I need to get the grading done, and I just can't seem to make it happen. So, yeah, I don't feel panicked about it or anything, just really irritated that I keep wasting my own time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. I mean, teaching teenagers can be a lot sometimes, but it's just the normal amount of stress that comes with the job. I don't feel like I'm snapping at people or getting upset over little things that shouldn't matter. It's really just that frustration with my own focus that I mentioned, but my mood otherwise is pretty stable.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering off like this when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm doing something I actually enjoy, like hanging out with friends or working on a hobby, I don't have that problem. I can pay attention just fine. I don't really zone out then. It seems to mostly just happen when I have to force myself to do something tedious, like the grading.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks. Well, I came in because I'm having a lot of trouble just... staying focused on my work. I teach high school, so there's a lot of grading and planning. But I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to get it done, and within ten minutes I realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page. My mind wanders off to three other things, and I barely take in what’s right in front of me. It's getting really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to pay attention to details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I haven't really noticed that being an issue. If I can actually get myself to read a student's paper, I catch the grammar mistakes and all the little things just fine. It's really just the... the staying engaged part that's hard, not the details themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I sit at my desk for hours trying to get through my grading. I don't feel like I need to get up and move around or anything like that. I'm usually pretty still, it's just my brain that's wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're unable to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wouldn't say I feel anxious or worried. It's more just... frustration. I get annoyed with myself because I know I need to get the grading done, and I just can't seem to make it happen. So, yeah, I don't feel panicked about it or anything, just really irritated that I keep wasting my own time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. I mean, teaching teenagers can be a lot sometimes, but it's just the normal amount of stress that comes with the job. I don't feel like I'm snapping at people or getting upset over little things that shouldn't matter. It's really just that frustration with my own focus that I mentioned, but my mood otherwise is pretty stable.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering off like this when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm doing something I actually enjoy, like hanging out with friends or working on a hobby, I don't have that problem. I can pay attention just fine. I don't really zone out then. It seems to mostly just happen when I have to force myself to do something tedious, like the grading.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time focusing on your work if you were more interested in the subject matter or enjoyed it more?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I actually do like the subject I teach, and being in the classroom with the kids is fine. I don't think being more interested in the material would change much about the paperwork side of things, though. \n\nGrading is just... well, it's grading. It's repetitive no matter how much you like the topic. So, yeah, I don't really know if that would make a difference for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on your work if you were being rewarded or recognized for it?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, we get recognized at work sometimes, like Teacher of the Month or whatever, but that doesn't really change how hard it is to sit down and actually get through a stack of essays. The reward doesn't make the task itself any less tedious. So, no, I don't think a reward would suddenly make me able to focus on it better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your ability to focus is worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a pattern like that, to be honest. I mean... it's pretty much just a struggle whenever I have to sit down and actually do the paperwork. I don't do a lot of other quiet, solitary things. Most of my day is spent around people, teaching or dealing with students. When I'm not doing that, I'm usually just trying to catch up on grading. I guess I don't really have any hobbies right now that would require me to just sit quietly and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus better on tasks like grading if you felt like they were more meaningful or contributed to a larger goal?\n\nPatient: I mean, grading *is* meaningful. It's how I know if my students are actually learning what I'm teaching them, and it's a big part of my job. I know it matters. But knowing that doesn't make it any easier to actually do it. The goal is there, but when I'm staring at the paper, the task itself is still just as hard to get through. My mind wanders off to three other things, and I barely take in what’s right in front of me. It's getting really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to pay attention to details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I haven't really noticed that being an issue. If I can actually get myself to read a student's paper, I catch the grammar mistakes and all the little things just fine. I mean, I sit at my desk for hours trying to get through my grading. I don't feel like I need to get up and move around or anything like that. It's really just the... the staying engaged part that's hard, not the details themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I sit at my desk for hours trying to get through my grading. I don't feel like I need to get up and move around or anything like that. I'm usually pretty still, it's just my brain that's wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're unable to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wouldn't say I feel anxious or worried. It's more just... frustration. I get annoyed with myself because I know I need to get the grading done, and I just can't seem to make it happen. So, yeah, I don't feel panicked about it or anything, just really irritated that I keep wasting my own time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. I mean, teaching teenagers can be a lot sometimes, but it's just the normal amount of stress that comes with the job. I don't feel like I'm snapping at people or getting upset over little things that shouldn't matter. It's really just that frustration with my own focus that I mentioned, but my mood otherwise is pretty stable.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering off like this when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm doing something I actually enjoy, like hanging out with friends or working on a hobby, I don't have that problem. I can pay attention just fine. I don't really zone out then. It seems to mostly just happen when I have to force myself to do something tedious, like the grading.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time focusing on your work if you were more interested in the subject matter or enjoyed it more?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I actually do like the subject I teach, and being in the classroom with the kids is fine. I don't think being more interested in the material would change much about the paperwork side of things, though. \n\nGrading is just... well, it's grading. It's repetitive no matter how much you like the topic. So, yeah, I don't really know if that would make a difference for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on your work if you were being rewarded or recognized for it?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, we get recognized at work sometimes, like Teacher of the Month or whatever, but that doesn't really change how hard it is to sit down and actually get through a stack of essays. The reward doesn't make the task itself any less tedious. So, no, I don't think a reward would suddenly make me able to focus on it better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your ability to focus is worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a pattern like that, to be honest. I mean... it's pretty much just a struggle whenever I have to sit down and actually do the paperwork. Whether I try to tackle it during my planning period in the morning or at home in the evening, it feels exactly the same. So, no, I wouldn't say it's worse at any particular time of day.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing in other quiet, solitary tasks outside of grading and work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't do a lot of other quiet, solitary things. Most of my day is spent around people, teaching or dealing with students. When I'm not doing that, I'm usually just trying to catch up on grading. I guess I don't really have any hobbies right now that would require me to just sit quietly and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus better on tasks like grading if you felt like they were more meaningful or contributed to a larger goal?\n\nPatient: I mean, grading *is* meaningful. It's how I know if my students are actually learning what I'm teaching them, and it's a big part of my job. I know it matters. But knowing that doesn't make it any easier to actually do it. The goal is there, but when I'm staring at the paper, the task itself is still just as hard to get through. So, no, I don't think feeling like it's part of a larger goal changes my ability to focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're doing tasks at home that are not related to work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Honestly, when I'm at home and not working... I don't really do much that takes a lot of focus. It's mostly just basic chores, like throwing a load of laundry in or making dinner, and those don't really require me to sit down and concentrate. Or I'm just watching TV to unwind. So, I haven't really noticed any issues with that, just because I don't really have many tasks at home outside of my teaching stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty focusing when you're reading for pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don't really read for pleasure, to be honest. By the time I'm done with the school day and whatever grading I can force myself to get through, I'm just too burned out. I usually just turn on the TV or something where I don't have to think. So I can't really say if I'd have trouble focusing on a book, because I haven't tried to sit down and read one in a long time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's really just the... the staying engaged part that's hard, not the details themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I sit at my desk for hours trying to get through my grading. I don't feel like I need to get up and move around or anything like that. I'm usually pretty still, it's just my brain that's wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're unable to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wouldn't say I feel anxious or worried. It's more just... frustration. I get annoyed with myself because I know I need to get the grading done, and I just can't seem to make it happen. So, yeah, I don't feel panicked about it or anything, just really irritated that I keep wasting my own time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. I mean, teaching teenagers can be a lot sometimes, but it's just the normal amount of stress that comes with the job. I don't feel like I'm snapping at people or getting upset over little things that shouldn't matter. It's really just that frustration with my own focus that I mentioned, but my mood otherwise is pretty stable.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering off like this when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm doing something I actually enjoy, like hanging out with friends or working on a hobby, I don't have that problem. I can pay attention just fine. I don't really zone out then. It seems to mostly just happen when I have to force myself to do something tedious, like the grading.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time focusing on your work if you were more interested in the subject matter or enjoyed it more?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I actually do like the subject I teach, and being in the classroom with the kids is fine. I don't think being more interested in the material would change much about the paperwork side of things, though. \n\nGrading is just... well, it's grading. It's repetitive no matter how much you like the topic. So, yeah, I don't really know if that would make a difference for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on your work if you were being rewarded or recognized for it?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, we get recognized at work sometimes, like Teacher of the Month or whatever, but that doesn't really change how hard it is to sit down and actually get through a stack of essays. The reward doesn't make the task itself any less tedious. So, no, I don't think a reward would suddenly make me able to focus on it better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your ability to focus is worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a pattern like that, to be honest. I mean... it's pretty much just a struggle whenever I have to sit down and actually do the paperwork. Whether I try to tackle it during my planning period in the morning or at home in the evening, it feels exactly the same. So, no, I wouldn't say it's worse at any particular time of day.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing in other quiet, solitary tasks outside of grading and work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't do a lot of other quiet, solitary things. Most of my day is spent around people, teaching or dealing with students. When I'm not doing that, I'm usually just trying to catch up on grading. I guess I don't really have any hobbies right now that would require me to just sit quietly and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus better on tasks like grading if you felt like they were more meaningful or contributed to a larger goal?\n\nPatient: I mean, grading *is* meaningful. It's how I know if my students are actually learning what I'm teaching them, and it's a big part of my job. I know it matters. But knowing that doesn't make it any easier to actually do it. The goal is there, but when I'm staring at the paper, the task itself is still just as hard to get through. So, no, I don't think feeling like it's part of a larger goal changes my ability to focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're doing tasks at home that are not related to work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Honestly, when I'm at home and not working... I don't really do much that takes a lot of focus. It's mostly just basic chores, like throwing a load of laundry in or making dinner, and those don't really require me to sit down and concentrate. Or I'm just watching TV to unwind. So, I haven't really noticed any issues with that, just because I don't really have many tasks at home outside of my teaching stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty focusing when you're reading for pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don't really read for pleasure, to be honest. By the time I'm done with the school day and whatever grading I can force myself to get through, I'm just too burned out. I usually just turn on the TV or something where I don't have to think. So I can't really say if I'd have trouble focusing on a book, because I haven't tried to sit down and read one in a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues because of your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. Um, at work, my missed follow-through has actually gotten serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan. So that's obviously an issue there with my colleagues and bosses. \n\nAnd at home... well, it affects my marriage, too. My wife has basically just started taking over all the bills and the household reminders because she... she just can’t really count on me to keep track of them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you're having trouble focusing on your work?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel sad or depressed. It's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I know what I need to do, and I just can't seem to get it done. But it doesn't make me feel down or hopeless or anything like that. Just annoyed with myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So, no, I wouldn't say it's worse at any particular time of day.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing in other quiet, solitary tasks outside of grading and work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't do a lot of other quiet, solitary things. Most of my day is spent around people, teaching or dealing with students. When I'm not doing that, I'm usually just trying to catch up on grading. I guess I don't really have any hobbies right now that would require me to just sit quietly and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus better on tasks like grading if you felt like they were more meaningful or contributed to a larger goal?\n\nPatient: I mean, grading *is* meaningful. It's how I know if my students are actually learning what I'm teaching them, and it's a big part of my job. I know it matters. But knowing that doesn't make it any easier to actually do it. The goal is there, but when I'm staring at the paper, the task itself is still just as hard to get through. So, no, I don't think feeling like it's part of a larger goal changes my ability to focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're doing tasks at home that are not related to work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Honestly, when I'm at home and not working... I don't really do much that takes a lot of focus. It's mostly just basic chores, like throwing a load of laundry in or making dinner, and those don't really require me to sit down and concentrate. Or I'm just watching TV to unwind. So, I haven't really noticed any issues with that, just because I don't really have many tasks at home outside of my teaching stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty focusing when you're reading for pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don't really read for pleasure, to be honest. By the time I'm done with the school day and whatever grading I can force myself to get through, I'm just too burned out. I usually just turn on the TV or something where I don't have to think. So I can't really say if I'd have trouble focusing on a book, because I haven't tried to sit down and read one in a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues because of your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. Um, at work, my missed follow-through has actually gotten serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan. So that's obviously an issue there with my colleagues and bosses. \n\nAnd at home... well, it affects my marriage, too. My wife has basically just started taking over all the bills and the household reminders because she... she just can’t really count on me to keep track of them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you're having trouble focusing on your work?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel sad or depressed. It's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I know what I need to do, and I just can't seem to get it done. But it doesn't make me feel down or hopeless or anything like that. Just annoyed with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset when you think about not being able to complete your work tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really overwhelmed or upset, no. Like I said, it's just frustrating. I get annoyed that I'm behind on grading or that I missed a deadline, but it doesn't make me feel panicked or anything. It's just a constant hassle that I have to deal with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks. Well, I came in because I'm having a lot of trouble just... staying focused on my work. I teach high school, so there's a lot of grading and planning. But I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to get it done, and within ten minutes I realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page. My mind wanders off to three other things, and I barely take in what’s right in front of me. It's getting really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to pay attention to details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I haven't really noticed that being an issue. If I can actually get myself to read a student's paper, I catch the grammar mistakes and all the little things just fine. It's really just the... the staying engaged part that's hard, not the details themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I sit at my desk for hours trying to get through my grading. I don't feel like I need to get up and move around or anything like that. I'm usually pretty still, it's just my brain that's wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're unable to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wouldn't say I feel anxious or worried. It's more just... frustration. I get annoyed with myself because I know I need to get the grading done, and I just can't seem to make it happen. So, yeah, I don't feel panicked about it or anything, just really irritated that I keep wasting my own time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. I mean, teaching teenagers can be a lot sometimes, but it's just the normal amount of stress that comes with the job. I don't feel like I'm snapping at people or getting upset over little things that shouldn't matter. It's really just that frustration with my own focus that I mentioned, but my mood otherwise is pretty stable.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering off like this when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm doing something I actually enjoy, like hanging out with friends or working on a hobby, I don't have that problem. I can pay attention just fine. I don't really zone out then. It seems to mostly just happen when I have to force myself to do something tedious, like the grading.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time focusing on your work if you were more interested in the subject matter or enjoyed it more?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I actually do like the subject I teach, and being in the classroom with the kids is fine. I don't think being more interested in the material would change much about the paperwork side of things, though. \n\nGrading is just... well, it's grading. It's repetitive no matter how much you like the topic. So, yeah, I don't really know if that would make a difference for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on your work if you were being rewarded or recognized for it?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, we get recognized at work sometimes, like Teacher of the Month or whatever, but that doesn't really change how hard it is to sit down and actually get through a stack of essays. The reward doesn't make the task itself any less tedious. So, no, I don't think a reward would suddenly make me able to focus on it better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your ability to focus is worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a pattern like that, to be honest. So I can't really say if I'd have trouble focusing on a book, because I haven't tried to sit down and read one in a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues because of your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. Um, at work, my missed follow-through has actually gotten serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan. So that's obviously an issue there with my colleagues and bosses. \n\nAnd at home... well, it affects my marriage, too. My wife has basically just started taking over all the bills and the household reminders because she... she just can’t really count on me to keep track of them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you're having trouble focusing on your work?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel sad or depressed. It's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I know what I need to do, and I just can't seem to get it done. But it doesn't make me feel down or hopeless or anything like that. Just annoyed with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset when you think about not being able to complete your work tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really overwhelmed or upset, no. Like I said, it's just frustrating. I get annoyed that I'm behind on grading or that I missed a deadline, but it doesn't make me feel panicked or anything. It's just a constant hassle that I have to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing on tasks, or is this a relatively new issue for you?\n\nPatient: No, it's definitely not a new issue. I've pretty much always been like this. Even back in primary school, my report cards used to say things like, uh, \"bright but careless,\" or that I wouldn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. And my parents were constantly finding worksheets just crumpled up at the bottom of my backpack that I had completely forgotten to turn in. So yeah, it's been a lifelong thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks. Well, I came in because I'm having a lot of trouble just... staying focused on my work. I teach high school, so there's a lot of grading and planning. But I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to get it done, and within ten minutes I realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page. My mind wanders off to three other things, and I barely take in what’s right in front of me. It's getting really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to pay attention to details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I haven't really noticed that being an issue. If I can actually get myself to read a student's paper, I catch the grammar mistakes and all the little things just fine. It's really just the... the staying engaged part that's hard, not the details themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I sit at my desk for hours trying to get through my grading. I don't feel like I need to get up and move around or anything like that. I'm usually pretty still, it's just my brain that's wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're unable to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wouldn't say I feel anxious or worried. It's more just... frustration. I get annoyed with myself because I know I need to get the grading done, and I just can't seem to make it happen. So, yeah, I don't feel panicked about it or anything, just really irritated that I keep wasting my own time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. I mean, teaching teenagers can be a lot sometimes, but it's just the normal amount of stress that comes with the job. I don't feel like I'm snapping at people or getting upset over little things that shouldn't matter. It's really just that frustration with my own focus that I mentioned, but my mood otherwise is pretty stable.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering off like this when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm doing something I actually enjoy, like hanging out with friends or working on a hobby, I don't have that problem. I can pay attention just fine. I don't really zone out then. It seems to mostly just happen when I have to force myself to do something tedious, like the grading.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time focusing on your work if you were more interested in the subject matter or enjoyed it more?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I actually do like the subject I teach, and being in the classroom with the kids is fine. I don't think being more interested in the material would change much about the paperwork side of things, though. \n\nGrading is just... well, it's grading. It's repetitive no matter how much you like the topic. So, yeah, I don't really know if that would make a difference for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on your work if you were being rewarded or recognized for it?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, we get recognized at work sometimes, like Teacher of the Month or whatever, but that doesn't really change how hard it is to sit down and actually get through a stack of essays. The reward doesn't make the task itself any less tedious. So, no, I don't think a reward would suddenly make me able to focus on it better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your ability to focus is worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a pattern like that, to be honest. I mean... it's pretty much just a struggle whenever I have to sit down and actually do the paperwork. Whether I try to tackle it during my planning period in the morning or at home in the evening, it feels exactly the same. So, no, I wouldn't say it's worse at any particular time of day.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing in other quiet, solitary tasks outside of grading and work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't do a lot of other quiet, solitary things. Most of my day is spent around people, teaching or dealing with students. When I'm not doing that, I'm usually just trying to catch up on grading. I guess I don't really have any hobbies right now that would require me to just sit quietly and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus better on tasks like grading if you felt like they were more meaningful or contributed to a larger goal?\n\nPatient: I mean, grading *is* meaningful. It's how I know if my students are actually learning what I'm teaching them, and it's a big part of my job. I know it matters. But knowing that doesn't make it any easier to actually do it. The goal is there, but when I'm staring at the paper, the task itself is still just as hard to get through. So, no, I don't think feeling like it's part of a larger goal changes my ability to focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're doing tasks at home that are not related to work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Honestly, when I'm at home and not working... I don't really do much that takes a lot of focus. It's mostly just basic chores, like throwing a load of laundry in or making dinner, and those don't really require me to sit down and concentrate. Or I'm just watching TV to unwind. So, I haven't really noticed any issues with that, just because I don't really have many tasks at home outside of my teaching stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty focusing when you're reading for pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don't really read for pleasure, to be honest.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks. Well, I came in because I'm having a lot of trouble just... staying focused on my work. I teach high school, so there's a lot of grading and planning. But I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to get it done, and within ten minutes I realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page. My mind wanders off to three other things, and I barely take in what’s right in front of me. It's getting really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to pay attention to details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I haven't really noticed that being an issue. If I can actually get myself to read a student's paper, I catch the grammar mistakes and all the little things just fine. It's really just the... the staying engaged part that's hard, not the details themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I sit at my desk for hours trying to get through my grading. I don't feel like I need to get up and move around or anything like that. I'm usually pretty still, it's just my brain that's wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're unable to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wouldn't say I feel anxious or worried. It's more just... frustration. I get annoyed with myself because I know I need to get the grading done, and I just can't seem to make it happen. So, yeah, I don't feel panicked about it or anything, just really irritated that I keep wasting my own time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. I mean, teaching teenagers can be a lot sometimes, but it's just the normal amount of stress that comes with the job. I don't feel like I'm snapping at people or getting upset over little things that shouldn't matter. It's really just that frustration with my own focus that I mentioned, but my mood otherwise is pretty stable.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering off like this when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm doing something I actually enjoy, like hanging out with friends or working on a hobby, I don't have that problem. I can pay attention just fine. I don't really zone out then. It seems to mostly just happen when I have to force myself to do something tedious, like the grading.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time focusing on your work if you were more interested in the subject matter or enjoyed it more?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I actually do like the subject I teach, and being in the classroom with the kids is fine. I don't think being more interested in the material would change much about the paperwork side of things, though. \n\nGrading is just... well, it's grading. It's repetitive no matter how much you like the topic. So, yeah, I don't really know if that would make a difference for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on your work if you were being rewarded or recognized for it?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, we get recognized at work sometimes, like Teacher of the Month or whatever, but that doesn't really change how hard it is to sit down and actually get through a stack of essays. The reward doesn't make the task itself any less tedious. So, no, I don't think a reward would suddenly make me able to focus on it better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your ability to focus is worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a pattern like that, to be honest. I mean... it's pretty much just a struggle whenever I have to sit down and actually do the paperwork. Whether I try to tackle it during my planning period in the morning or at home in the evening, it feels exactly the same. So, no, I wouldn't say it's worse at any particular time of day.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing in other quiet, solitary tasks outside of grading and work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't do a lot of other quiet, solitary things. Most of my day is spent around people, teaching or dealing with students. When I'm not doing that, I'm usually just trying to catch up on grading. I guess I don't really have any hobbies right now that would require me to just sit quietly and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus better on tasks like grading if you felt like they were more meaningful or contributed to a larger goal?\n\nPatient: I mean, grading *is* meaningful. It's how I know if my students are actually learning what I'm teaching them, and it's a big part of my job. I know it matters. But knowing that doesn't make it any easier to actually do it. The goal is there, but when I'm staring at the paper, the task itself is still just as hard to get through. So, no, I don't think feeling like it's part of a larger goal changes my ability to focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're doing tasks at home that are not related to work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Honestly, when I'm at home and not working... I don't really do much that takes a lot of focus. It's mostly just basic chores, like throwing a load of laundry in or making dinner, and those don't really require me to sit down and concentrate. Or I'm just watching TV to unwind. So, I haven't really noticed any issues with that, just because I don't really have many tasks at home outside of my teaching stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty focusing when you're reading for pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don't really read for pleasure, to be honest. By the time I'm done with the school day and whatever grading I can force myself to get through, I'm just too burned out. I usually just turn on the TV or something where I don't have to think. So I can't really say if I'd have trouble focusing on a book, because I haven't tried to sit down and read one in a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues because of your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. Um, at work, my missed follow-through has actually gotten serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan. So that's obviously an issue there with my colleagues and bosses. \n\nAnd at home... well, it affects my marriage, too. My wife has basically just started taking over all the bills and the household reminders because she... she just can’t really count on me to keep track of them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you're having trouble focusing on your work?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel sad or depressed. It's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I know what I need to do, and I just can't seem to get it done. But it doesn't make me feel down or hopeless or anything like that. Just annoyed with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset when you think about not being able to complete your work tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really overwhelmed or upset, no. Like I said, it's just frustrating. I get annoyed that I'm behind on grading or that I missed a deadline, but it doesn't make me feel panicked or anything. It's just a constant hassle that I have to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing on tasks, or is this a relatively new issue for you?\n\nPatient: No, it's definitely not a new issue. I've pretty much always been like this. Even back in primary school, my report cards used to say things like, uh, \"bright but careless,\" or that I wouldn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. But even when it's the weekend and I'm doing something I actually want to do, I still find myself getting sidetracked or leaving things half-finished. So, yeah, I don't really know if wanting to do it changes my focus all that much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing is more because you're not interested in the tasks or because you're having trouble regulating your attention?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really thought about it in those exact terms. Um... I guess, since it happens even when I'm doing things I actually like, maybe it's more about having trouble controlling it? But I really couldn't say for sure. I just know that no matter what I'm doing, my mind just kind of wanders off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I've pretty much always been like this. Even back in primary school, my report cards used to say things like, uh, \"bright but careless,\" or that I wouldn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. So yeah, it's been a lifelong thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus better if you were doing something you really wanted to do, rather than something you feel you have to do?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't really noticed a big difference. I mean, maybe? So, yeah, I don't really know if wanting to do it changes my focus all that much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing is more because you're not interested in the tasks or because you're having trouble regulating your attention?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. But I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to get it done, and within ten minutes I realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page. My mind wanders off to three other things, and I barely take in what’s right in front of me. It's getting really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to pay attention to details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I haven't really noticed that being an issue. If I can actually get myself to read a student's paper, I catch the grammar mistakes and all the little things just fine. It's really just the... the staying engaged part that's hard, not the details themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I sit at my desk for hours trying to get through my grading. I don't feel like I need to get up and move around or anything like that. I'm usually pretty still, it's just my brain that's wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're unable to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wouldn't say I feel anxious or worried. It's more just... frustration. I get annoyed with myself because I know I need to get the grading done, and I just can't seem to make it happen. So, yeah, I don't feel panicked about it or anything, just really irritated that I keep wasting my own time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. I mean, teaching teenagers can be a lot sometimes, but it's just the normal amount of stress that comes with the job. I don't feel like I'm snapping at people or getting upset over little things that shouldn't matter. It's really just that frustration with my own focus that I mentioned, but my mood otherwise is pretty stable.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering off like this when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm doing something I actually enjoy, like hanging out with friends or working on a hobby, I don't have that problem. I can pay attention just fine. I don't really zone out then. It seems to mostly just happen when I have to force myself to do something tedious, like the grading.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time focusing on your work if you were more interested in the subject matter or enjoyed it more?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I actually do like the subject I teach, and being in the classroom with the kids is fine. I don't think being more interested in the material would change much about the paperwork side of things, though. \n\nGrading is just... well, it's grading. It's repetitive no matter how much you like the topic. So, yeah, I don't really know if that would make a difference for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus on your work if you were being rewarded or recognized for it?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, we get recognized at work sometimes, like Teacher of the Month or whatever, but that doesn't really change how hard it is to sit down and actually get through a stack of essays. The reward doesn't make the task itself any less tedious. So, no, I don't think a reward would suddenly make me able to focus on it better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your ability to focus is worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a pattern like that, to be honest. I mean... it's pretty much just a struggle whenever I have to sit down and actually do the paperwork. Whether I try to tackle it during my planning period in the morning or at home in the evening, it feels exactly the same. So, no, I wouldn't say it's worse at any particular time of day.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing in other quiet, solitary tasks outside of grading and work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't do a lot of other quiet, solitary things. Most of my day is spent around people, teaching or dealing with students. When I'm not doing that, I'm usually just trying to catch up on grading. I guess I don't really have any hobbies right now that would require me to just sit quietly and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus better on tasks like grading if you felt like they were more meaningful or contributed to a larger goal?\n\nPatient: I mean, grading *is* meaningful. It's how I know if my students are actually learning what I'm teaching them, and it's a big part of my job. I know it matters. But knowing that doesn't make it any easier to actually do it. The goal is there, but when I'm staring at the paper, the task itself is still just as hard to get through. So, no, I don't think feeling like it's part of a larger goal changes my ability to focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're doing tasks at home that are not related to work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Honestly, when I'm at home and not working... I don't really do much that takes a lot of focus. It's mostly just basic chores, like throwing a load of laundry in or making dinner, and those don't really require me to sit down and concentrate. Or I'm just watching TV to unwind. So, I haven't really noticed any issues with that, just because I don't really have many tasks at home outside of my teaching stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty focusing when you're reading for pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don't really read for pleasure, to be honest. By the time I'm done with the school day and whatever grading I can force myself to get through, I'm just too burned out. I usually just turn on the TV or something where I don't have to think. So I can't really say if I'd have trouble focusing on a book, because I haven't tried to sit down and read one in a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues because of your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. Um, at work, my missed follow-through has actually gotten serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan. It's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I know what I need to do, and I just can't seem to get it done. But it doesn't make me feel down or hopeless or anything like that. Just annoyed with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset when you think about not being able to complete your work tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really overwhelmed or upset, no. I don't do a lot of other quiet, solitary things. Most of my day is spent around people, teaching or dealing with students. When I'm not doing that, I'm usually just trying to catch up on grading. I guess I don't really have any hobbies right now that would require me to just sit quietly and focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus better on tasks like grading if you felt like they were more meaningful or contributed to a larger goal?\n\nPatient: I mean, grading *is* meaningful. It's how I know if my students are actually learning what I'm teaching them, and it's a big part of my job. I know it matters. But knowing that doesn't make it any easier to actually do it. The goal is there, but when I'm staring at the paper, the task itself is still just as hard to get through. So, no, I don't think feeling like it's part of a larger goal changes my ability to focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're doing tasks at home that are not related to work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Honestly, when I'm at home and not working... I don't really do much that takes a lot of focus. It's mostly just basic chores, like throwing a load of laundry in or making dinner, and those don't really require me to sit down and concentrate. Or I'm just watching TV to unwind. So, I haven't really noticed any issues with that, just because I don't really have many tasks at home outside of my teaching stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty focusing when you're reading for pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don't really read for pleasure, to be honest. By the time I'm done with the school day and whatever grading I can force myself to get through, I'm just too burned out. I usually just turn on the TV or something where I don't have to think. So I can't really say if I'd have trouble focusing on a book, because I haven't tried to sit down and read one in a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues because of your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. Um, at work, my missed follow-through has actually gotten serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan. So that's obviously an issue there with my colleagues and bosses. \n\nAnd at home... well, it affects my marriage, too. My wife has basically just started taking over all the bills and the household reminders because she... she just can’t really count on me to keep track of them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you're having trouble focusing on your work?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel sad or depressed. It's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I know what I need to do, and I just can't seem to get it done. But it doesn't make me feel down or hopeless or anything like that. Just annoyed with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or upset when you think about not being able to complete your work tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really overwhelmed or upset, no. Like I said, it's just frustrating. I get annoyed that I'm behind on grading or that I missed a deadline, but it doesn't make me feel panicked or anything. It's just a constant hassle that I have to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing on tasks, or is this a relatively new issue for you?\n\nPatient: No, it's definitely not a new issue. I've pretty much always been like this. Even back in primary school, my report cards used to say things like, uh, \"bright but careless,\" or that I wouldn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. And my parents were constantly finding worksheets just crumpled up at the bottom of my backpack that I had completely forgotten to turn in. So yeah, it's been a lifelong thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to focus better if you were doing something you really wanted to do, rather than something you feel you have to do?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't really noticed a big difference. I mean, maybe? But even when it's the weekend and I'm doing something I actually want to do, I still find myself getting sidetracked or leaving things half-finished. So, yeah, I don't really know if wanting to do it changes my focus all that much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing is more because you're not interested in the tasks or because you're having trouble regulating your attention?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really thought about it in those exact terms. Um... I guess, since it happens even when I'm doing things I actually like, maybe it's more about having trouble controlling it? But I really couldn't say for sure. I just know that no matter what I'm doing, my mind just kind of wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders off because of the task you're doing or because you just can't seem to keep your attention on anything for a long time?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. It's hard to tell the difference, to be honest. I just know that whether I'm grading papers for my classes or trying to read a book I bought for myself, I end up losing my place or thinking about something else. So I'm not sure if it's the task itself or just my attention span giving out."
